 \tag{LB6}
\]
To prove it, stop to bound the bracket and total variation, take
expectations in It\^o's formula for $Z^2$, and then use Fatou.
The resulting finite expected bracket also makes the martingale
square integrable, so the usual unstopped identity is justified.
Apply (LB6) first to $W$ and use (LB3) and (LB2):
\[
 \widehat V\le P V+\tfrac12Q\mathbb E C_\theta+3QT,
\]
which gives (LB4). In the opposite direction put
$\widetilde C_t=\widehat C_{u(t)}$ and solve (LB3):
\[
 dA=p^{-1}dD-\frac{q}{2p^3}d\widetilde C+3q p^{-1}dt.
\]
Apply (LB6) to $Y$ and use (LB2) to obtain (LB5). These estimates
are invoked with the source-side budget finite; the corresponding
conclusion then guarantees finiteness on the other side. The equality
between budgets and expected variation is the earlier exact
conditional-variation identity, applied to the matched stopped
processes. A budget in the original capacity and one in the deleted
capacity must retain their inherited stopping rules.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Two deletion directions eliminate singular characteristics]
\label{prop:two-deletion-measures}
Take two admissible deletion maps $h_t^+,h_t^-$ on the same initial
trace. Suppose their drivers are continuous semimartingales. Define
$p^\pm,q^\pm,u_\pm$ as in (LB1), let
$D^\pm_t=\widehat A^\pm_{u_\pm(t)}$, and suppose
\[
 \Delta_t=p_t^+q_t^- -p_t^-q_t^+\ne0
 \tag{LB7}
\]
on a positive initial interval. Then the original driver is a
semimartingale and its characteristic measures satisfy
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 dA&=\frac{q^-\,dD^+-q^+\,dD^-}{\Delta},\\
 dC-6dt&=\frac{2(p^+\,dD^- -p^-\,dD^+)}{\Delta}.
 \end{aligned}}
 \tag{LB8}
\]
No initial derivatives or drift densities are required. In particular,
if both deleted drift measures are absolutely continuous in their
capacity clocks, then the original drift and bracket are absolutely
continuous on this interval. The original drift need not have been
assumed absolutely continuous.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The preceding proposition transfers semimartingality from either
deleted chart to the original one. Apply (LB3) to both, and invert the
two-by-two system of signed measures. This gives (LB8). The clocks
$du_\pm=(p^\pm)^2dt$ are locally bi-Lipschitz after localization, so
absolute continuity of a deleted drift in its own capacity is exactly
absolute continuity of its pullback $D^\pm$ in $t$. Equation (LB8)
then proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The singular-part alternative is exact.}
Take pathwise Lebesgue decompositions with respect to $dt$. Writing
superscript $\perp$ for singular Stieltjes parts, (LB3) says
\[
 dD^\perp=p\,dA^\perp+\tfrac12q\,dC^\perp,
 \qquad d\widetilde C^\perp=p^2\,dC^\perp.
 \tag{LB9}
\]
Thus absence of a singular bracket is invariant under analytic local
coordinate changes. Once the bracket is absolutely continuous,
absence of a singular drift is invariant as well. If the bracket has
a singular part, an It\^o correction can cancel that part from a
particular chart's drift; two distinct directions with (LB7) cannot
both cancel it. Formula (LB8) also gives the pathwise measure bounds
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |dA|&\le\frac{|q^-|\,|dD^+|+|q^+|\,|dD^-|}{|\Delta|},\\
 |dC-6dt|&\le\frac{2p^+|dD^-|+2p^-|dD^+|}{|\Delta|}.
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{LB10}
\]
These also hold for singular parts, with the second left side replaced
by the positive measure $dC^\perp$.

For the mirrored shrinking slits of (DR6), initially
$p_0^\pm=d_\epsilon\asymp\epsilon$ and
$q_0^-=-q_0^+=-e_\epsilon^+$ with
$|e_\epsilon^+|\asymp\epsilon^{-1}$. The determinant is nonzero.
By stopping when the coefficients depart by a sufficiently small
relative amount from these initial values, (LB10) gives, with an
absolute numerical $K$ independent of small $\epsilon$,
\[
 |dA|\le K\epsilon^{-1}(|dD^+|+|dD^-|),\qquad
 |dC-6dt|\le K\epsilon(|dD^+|+|dD^-|).
 \tag{LB11}
\]
In particular singular bracket mass costs at least inverse-linear
singular drift mass across these two deleted charts. The matched
positive stopping interval may shrink with $\epsilon$. Therefore
finiteness of each deleted budget does not permit passing to
$\epsilon=0$ in (LB11) on a fixed interval. A quantitative uniform
budget estimate is still needed; no regularity or classification is
being deduced from bare locality by this calculation.

\paragraph{What this changes in the research target.}
Finiteness of a localized signed budget is a property of the local
physical source law within a fixed complex structure, rather than an
artifact of choosing one of its analytic deletion charts. Its numerical
size is not invariant: (LB3)--(LB5) exhibit the bracket correction and
small first derivative which can inflate it. Analytic coordinate changes
are being used throughout; an arbitrary real affine normalization is
not such a change. Thus this result neither selects the unknown affine
structure nor asserts semimartingality in all affine structures. The new
positive regularization is the two-direction elimination of singular
characteristics. Obtaining finite budgets and the quantitative scale
control required for Brownian rigidity remains open.

\subsection{Target-invariant force-point branches still fail source locality}
\label{sec:forcepoint-locality-audit}

A useful test is the familiar target-invariant branching
$\mathrm{SLE}_\kappa(\kappa-6)$ construction. Target invariance holds
within one physical domain; it does not assert invariance under deleting
remote pieces of that domain. The following calculation excludes the
standard one-sided force-point family from the present conjecture,
including the singular drift at an initially coincident force point.
It does not assume a finite initial drift tangent.

Use the convention
\[
 dW_t=\sqrt\kappa\,dB_t+\frac\rho{W_t-V_t}\,dt,
 \qquad dV_t=\frac2{V_t-W_t}\,dt,
 \qquad (W_0,V_0)=(0,0^+),\quad \rho>-2.
 \tag{FP1}
\]
The right force point is the boundary-prime-end limit immediately to
the right of the source, transported by the Loewner map. The gap
$(V-W)/\sqrt\kappa$ is a Bessel process of dimension
$\delta=1+2(\rho+2)/\kappa>1$. In particular, its zero set has Lebesgue
measure zero, and the force drift in (FP1) is locally integrable.
These standard statements follow from the Bessel construction; see
Sheffield~\cite{sheffield-cle}, Section 3.1, Propositions 3.1 and 3.3,
and Section 3.3.

\begin{proposition}[Only the ordinary SLE$_6$ member is source-local]
\label{prop:forcepoint-locality-exclusion}
Let $\kappa>0$. Consider the canonical conformally covariant family with one force point
immediately on a specified side of its source, defined by (FP1).
If it satisfies source-neighborhood locality under remote hull deletions,
then $\kappa=6$ and $\rho=0$.
In particular, for $4<\kappa<8$, the target-invariant
$\mathrm{SLE}_\kappa(\kappa-6)$ family fails that locality whenever
$\kappa\ne6$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $A$ be a fixed half-plane hull away from $0$, let
$h:\mathbb H\setminus A\to\mathbb H$ be its hydrodynamic map, and
compare the exploration in $\mathbb H$ with that in
$\mathbb H\setminus A$. Center the new source at $h(0)$.
Work up to a positive stopping time before the growing hull encounters
$A$, chosen so that the comparison maps are analytic across a fixed
interval containing $W_t$ and $V_t$. Let $h_t$ be the evolving deletion
map and $ds=h_t'(W_t)^2dt$. The centered transformed driver and force
point are $h_t(W_t)-h(0)$ and $h_t(V_t)-h(0)$.
The force point is transported exactly: the deletion-map PDE and (FP1)
give
\[
 d\{h_t(V_t)\}
 =\frac{2h_t'(W_t)^2}{h_t(V_t)-h_t(W_t)}\,dt.
 \tag{FP2}
\]
The driving change-of-coordinate calculation yields the Brownian part
$\sqrt\kappa\,d\widehat B_s$ and drift, in the original $dt$ clock,
\[
 \frac{\rho h_t'(W_t)}{W_t-V_t}
       +\left(\frac\kappa2-3\right)h_t''(W_t).
\]
For the force-point law in the deleted domain, the required drift in
that same clock is
\[
 \frac{\rho h_t'(W_t)^2}{h_t(W_t)-h_t(V_t)}.
\]
Therefore locality would require
\[
 \begin{split}
 R_t={}&\rho\left\{
 \frac{h_t'(W_t)}{W_t-V_t}
 -\frac{h_t'(W_t)^2}{h_t(W_t)-h_t(V_t)}\right\}
 +\frac{\kappa-6}{2}h_t''(W_t)=0
 \end{split}
 \tag{FP3}
\]
for Lebesgue-almost every sufficiently small $t$.
This follows from uniqueness of the canonical semimartingale drift:
the force point is the canonical one-sided source-boundary Loewner
flow, with its instant-reflecting continuation at collisions. It is
adapted to the driver and is transported as the specified boundary side,
not an independently reassignable mark. The
capacity change is invertible on the stopped comparison interval.
All drift terms are integrable since $\delta>1$.

For an analytic $H$ with $H'(w)>0$, put $d=w-v$. Taylor expansion gives
\[
 \frac{H'(w)}{d}
 -\frac{H'(w)^2}{H(w)-H(v)}
 =-\frac12H''(w)+\frac16 H'(w)\mathcal S H(w)\,d+O(d^2),
 \tag{FP4}
\]
where $\mathcal S H=H'''/H'-\tfrac32(H''/H')^2$ is the Schwarzian.
The remainder is uniform in a compact analytic neighborhood on which
$H'$ is bounded away from zero. Consequently
\[
 R_t=\frac{\kappa-6-\rho}{2}h_t''(W_t)
 +\frac\rho6 h_t'(W_t)\mathcal S h_t(W_t)(W_t-V_t)
 +O((W_t-V_t)^2).
 \tag{FP5}
\]
Choose, for example, the vertical slit
$A=\{x+iy:0\le y\le a\}$ with $x,a>0$. Its map is
$h(z)=x+\sqrt{(z-x)^2+a^2}$, with the hydrodynamic branch.
At $0$ both $h''(0)\ne0$ and $\mathcal S h(0)\ne0$. More explicitly,
writing $u=z-x$, the Schwarzian on the real analytic source interval is
\[
 \mathcal S h(z)
 =-\frac{3a^2(u^2+a^2/2)}{u^2(u^2+a^2)^2}<0.
 \tag{FP6}
\]
As $t\downarrow0$, $W_t,V_t\to0$ and the analytic maps $h_t$ converge
locally to $h$, with their derivatives. If $\kappa-6-\rho\ne0$, (FP5)
therefore makes $R_t$ bounded away from zero on a positive initial
interval, contradicting (FP3). Thus $\rho=\kappa-6$.

If this common value is nonzero, the second term of (FP5), divided by
$W_t-V_t$, tends to
$\rho h'(0)\mathcal S h(0)/6\ne0$. After decreasing the positive
comparison time, $R_t\ne0$ whenever $W_t\ne V_t$. The latter holds
for Lebesgue-almost every time by the Bessel zero-set statement, again
contradicting (FP3). Hence $\rho=0$ and $\kappa=6$.

Although written in half-plane coordinates, the test applies to bounded
physical domains: map the half-plane and the remote slit into a disk by
one fixed M\"obius map. The resulting physical domains agree near their
common source. The same calculation in their chordal charts then
contradicts source locality there.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Why target invariance does not contradict this exclusion.}
For a M\"obius $H$ one has the exact identity
\[
 \frac{H'(w)}{w-v}
 -\frac{H'(w)^2}{H(w)-H(v)}=-\frac12H''(w).
 \tag{FP7}
\]
Thus the force-point drift with $\rho=\kappa-6$ cancels the entire
coordinate anomaly for M\"obius changes of target. A remote domain
hull deletion instead has nonzero Schwarzian, leaving the second term
of (FP5). Schramm--Wilson~\cite{schramm-wilson}, Theorem 3, establishes the M\"obius coordinate-change theorem with sum
of force weights $\kappa-6$; it does not assert remote-domain locality:
\url{https://nyjm.albany.edu/nyjm/j/2005/11-31.pdf}.
Sheffield's Proposition 3.14 and Section 4.2 use target invariance to
construct branching radial explorations within one fixed domain.
Those statements are consistent with the calculation above.

\paragraph{There is also an extra-state issue for strong DMP.}
A force point started at $0^+$ is usually separated from the tip at
positive times. Conditional continuation then carries its current
boundary-prime-end image $V_t$, rather than restarting with a fresh
force point at $W_t^+$. The standard force-point Markov property is
therefore a property of the domain with this additional mark (and,
in side-swapping variants, the appropriate excursion state). It is not
by itself the required strong-DMP identity on a domain with only the
two colored boundary arcs and marked source/target. A proposed intrinsic
recovery of that mark from the colored domain would require its own
proof. This issue is independent of the locality failure already proved.

\paragraph{Consequence for the present research direction.}
Even if the desired RSW estimates were granted to these branches, they
would not be counterexamples to the current conjecture: the locality
test excludes them before RSW enters. They are nevertheless a useful
singular-drift test. Their Bessel force drift is integrable, so merely
obtaining semimartingality or finite aggregate signed variation would
not eliminate them. The exact locality comparison supplies information
beyond initial drift tangents; in this explicit ansatz it is the
Schwarzian remainder that distinguishes target independence from full
source locality.

The failure of source locality persists after any one fixed affine
change applied to the entire family: such a change preserves common
source neighborhoods and transports their stopped-law equality.


\section{Near-critical germ singularity and the energy of a driving drift}
\label{sec:germ-energy}

\paragraph{A stronger external obstruction.}
Aumann~\cite{aumann}, Theorem 2.1 and Corollary 2.2, proves mutual
singularity of distinct near-critical triangular-percolation interface
limits already on the raw initial ordered-germ sigma-field. The theorem
assumes a common subsequence with the stated quad-crossing convergence;
for comparison with the critical parameter zero, that condition follows
from Cardy's formula. Corollary 5.1 gives singularity under nontrivial
scalar dilations at a fixed nonzero parameter, again with its convergence
hypotheses. Thus a curve-level dilation obstruction is available, without
identifying random slit-domain continuation kernels. It does not construct
the coherent Borel strong-DMP family still needed for a counterexample to
the corrected three-axiom conjecture.

The following consequence concerns a proposed driving representation,
not a claim of semimartingality of a near-critical interface.

\begin{proposition}[Finite driving energy preserves the Brownian initial germ]
\label{prop:finite-energy-germ}
Let $X_0=0$ be continuous on $[0,T]$, and let $B$ be Brownian motion
in a usual filtration to which $X$ is adapted. Suppose $\kappa>0$, $\rho>0$
is a stopping time almost surely, and, for $t\le\rho\wedge T$,
\[
 X_t=\sqrt\kappa B_t+\int_0^t b_s\,ds,\qquad
 \int_0^{\rho\wedge T}b_s^2\,ds<\infty\quad\hbox{almost surely},
 \tag{GE1}
\]
where $b$ is progressively measurable. On the canonical continuous-path
space put $\mathcal H_{0+}=\bigcap_{t>0}\sigma(x_s:0\le s\le t)$,
using the raw sigma-fields. Then, for every $E\in\mathcal H_{0+}$,
\[
 \mathbb P\{X\in E\}=\mathbb W_\kappa(E)\in\{0,1\},
 \tag{GE2}
\]
where $\mathbb W_\kappa$ is Wiener measure of variance rate $\kappa$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Set
\[
 \sigma=\rho\wedge T\wedge
       \inf\left\{t:\int_0^{t\wedge\rho}b_s^2ds\ge\kappa\right\}.
\]
The finite energy in (GE1) implies $\sigma>0$ almost surely. Define
$\theta_s=\kappa^{-1/2}b_s\mathbf1_{\{s\le\sigma\}}$. Its integrated
square is at most one. Novikov's criterion and Girsanov's theorem
(for example~\cite{lalley-girsanov}, Theorems 1--2) therefore make
\[
 Z_T=\exp\left\{-\int_0^T\theta_s\,dB_s
                         -\frac12\int_0^T\theta_s^2ds\right\}
\]
a strictly positive density of an equivalent probability $Q$. Under $Q$,
\[
 \widetilde X_t=\sqrt\kappa B_t+\int_0^{t\wedge\sigma}b_sds
\]
is Brownian motion of variance rate $\kappa$ on $[0,T]$. The paths
$X$ and $\widetilde X$ agree on the strictly positive interval
$[0,\sigma]$. Every raw germ event has the same membership for two
paths agreeing on some positive initial interval: choose any deterministic
$1/n$ inside that interval and use measurability in
$\sigma(x_s:s\le1/n)$. Consequently
$Q\{X\in E\}=\mathbb W_\kappa(E)$. Blumenthal's zero--one law makes
the latter probability zero or one; equivalence of $P$ and $Q$ gives
(GE2). There is no global Novikov assumption on the original drift.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A near-critical limit cannot be a finite-energy drift of SLE]
\label{cor:nearcritical-energy}
Consider a nonzero near-critical interface limit covered by Aumann's
comparison with the critical limit. Assume it has a continuous
Loewner-generating parametrization, with an adapted Borel correspondence
between driver prefixes and ordered trace prefixes. Its driver in a
capacity chart where the critical comparison has law $\sqrt6 B$ cannot
satisfy (GE1) with $\kappa=6$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A raw ordered trace-germ event is unchanged when two nonconstant
Loewner traces agree for a positive capacity interval. In the preceding
proof the original and the $Q$-Brownian drivers have precisely such an
interval in common. The corresponding traces therefore have the same
raw germ-event membership. Under $Q$ the comparison trace is SLE$_6$;
its raw initial germ is zero--one (equivalently use the Brownian germ
and the adapted correspondence). Equivalence then gives the same germ
probabilities under the original law. This contradicts the germ event
separating the nonzero near-critical and critical laws in
Aumann's Corollary 2.2.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The energy must diverge immediately if such an AC representation exists.}
More precisely, suppose $X=\sqrt\kappa B+\int_0^\cdot b_sds$ on
$[0,T]$, with $b$ progressively measurable and integrable in absolute
value, and suppose a raw germ event $E$ has probability one for $X$
and zero under $\mathbb W_\kappa$. Then
\[
 \int_0^t b_s^2ds=\infty\quad\hbox{for every }0<t\le T,
       \quad\hbox{almost surely}.
 \tag{GE3}
\]
Indeed set $\sigma=T\wedge\inf\{t:\int_0^t b_s^2ds\ge\kappa\}$,
allowing $\sigma=0$ on instantaneous infinite energy. This is a stopping
time in the usual filtration. In all cases
$\int_0^\sigma b_s^2ds\le\kappa$, by monotone convergence from times
strictly below $\sigma$ when $\sigma>0$. The same bounded-energy
change of measure as above makes
$\widetilde X=\sqrt\kappa B+\int_0^{\cdot\wedge\sigma}b_sds$
Brownian under an equivalent probability. Thus $E(\widetilde X)$
has probability zero under both probabilities. On $\{\sigma>0\}$,
however, $X$ and $\widetilde X$ have the same germ, so $E(X)=1$
forces $\mathbb P(\sigma>0)=0$. Finite energy on any positive initial
interval would imply $\sigma>0$, proving (GE3). The identical argument
uses the trace-germ separator for the near-critical comparison.

\paragraph{Why square integrability cannot be weakened to absolute integrability.}
For $0<\alpha<1/2$, the process
$X_t=\sqrt\kappa B_t+t^\alpha$ has a continuous absolutely continuous
finite-variation drift, with density $\alpha t^{\alpha-1}$ integrable
at zero but with infinite square integral on every positive interval.
Brownian small-time H\"older regularity gives
$B_t/t^\alpha\to0$ almost surely. Hence $X_t/t^\alpha\to1$ almost
surely, while the corresponding Wiener limit is zero. This explicitly
separates their raw initial germs and shows why the energy hypothesis
in Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-energy-germ} is substantive.

\paragraph{Scope and compatibility with source shielding.}
This excludes Brownian driving with a locally bounded, or more generally
locally square-integrable, absolutely continuous drift. It does not prove
that a near-critical driver is not a semimartingale: a singular
finite-variation part, infinite drift energy at every positive initial
time, or a different martingale bracket is not excluded. Neither the
bracket-six premise nor Loewner generation is supplied by the singularity
theorem. Raw germ singularity between two laws is compatible with an
individual germ zero--one law for each: different laws may assign
different deterministic values to the same germ event. Source shielding
alone therefore cannot identify the universal germ law.


% Candidate independently checkable research fragment, 29 October 2026.
% Do not confuse original-prefix indexing with each deformed curve's own capacity.
\section{Affine deformation without assuming regular driving characteristics}
\label{sec:affine-beltrami-prefix}

\paragraph{Scope.}
The statements in this section are deterministic statements about bounded
half-plane hulls and continuously generated Loewner chains. No simple-curve,
zero-area, or semimartingale hypothesis is made. Boundary values of conformal
maps are interpreted at the distinguished tip prime end. A real-linear map
of the half-plane preserves filled hulls, and the transformed chain is
assumed to have its continuous capacity parametrization, as supplied by the
affine admissibility results for the model under consideration.

Let $g_t:\mathbb H\setminus K_t\to\mathbb H$ be hydrodynamically
normalized, $g_t(z)=z+2t/z+O(z^{-2})$, with continuous driver $W_t$, and put
$f_t=g_t^{-1}$. Let $L(z)=az+b\bar z$ be an orientation-preserving
real-linear self-map of $\mathbb H$. Write
\[
 s(t)=\tfrac12\operatorname{hcap}(LK_t),\qquad
 F_t=\widehat g_{s(t)}\circ L\circ f_t,\qquad
 U_t=F_t(W_t),
 \tag{AB1}
\]
where $\widehat g$ uniformizes the transformed hulls. Then
\[
 \mu_{F_t}(z)=\frac ba\,
       \frac{\overline{f_t'(z)}}{f_t'(z)}.
 \tag{AB2}
\]
For a model in which $L\gamma$ is SLE$_6$, $U_t=\sqrt6B_{s(t)}$.
This is an identity for a known model, not a classification consequence
of the exploration axioms.

\begin{proposition}[An interior Stieltjes evolution]
\label{prop:beltrami-stieltjes}
At every $z\in\mathbb H$, locally in finite time,
\[
 dF_t(z)=\frac{2\,ds(t)}{F_t(z)-U_t}
 -\left\{\frac{2(F_t)_z(z)}{z-W_t}
          +\frac{2(F_t)_{\bar z}(z)}{\bar z-W_t}\right\}dt.
 \tag{AB3}
\]
The equation is an equality of finite-variation measures, locally
uniformly on compact subsets of $\mathbb H$. It does not require that
$s$ be absolutely continuous. The field
$\mu_t=\mu_{F_t}$ satisfies, in the interior,
\[
 \partial_t\mu_t
 =-v_t\partial_z\mu_t-\overline{v_t}\partial_{\bar z}\mu_t
       +(v_t'-\overline{v_t'})\mu_t,
 \qquad v_t(z)=\frac2{z-W_t}.
 \tag{AB4}
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Inverse Loewner evolution gives $\partial_tf_t=-v_tf_t'$. Apply the
ordinary chain rule to (AB1), using the Loewner equation for
$\widehat g$ in its clock $s$ and the real-linear derivative of $L$.
The two contributions from $\partial_tf_t$ are exactly the two
$dt$ terms in (AB3). On any compact spatial set and finite localized
time interval, the images stay a positive distance from the real line;
the spatial derivatives and denominators are bounded. Since $s$ is
continuous increasing, the displayed terms have finite total variation.
For (AB4), differentiate
$\partial_tf_t'=-v_tf_t''-v_t'f_t'$
and its conjugate in (AB2). This calculation is entirely in the interior.
\end{proof}

Interior finite variation of $F$ does not justify applying It\^o's formula
to $F_t(W_t)$. Neither boundary differentiability of $F_t$ at $W_t$ nor
the necessary uniform boundary time regularity has been proved.

\subsection{A normalization-safe first variation at an arbitrary hull}

We use the boundary-fixed affine perturbation
\[
 L_\epsilon(w)=w+\epsilon\nu(\bar w-w),\qquad \nu\in\mathbb C,
 \quad \epsilon\in\mathbb R.
 \tag{AV1}
\]
For small $\epsilon$ it maps $\mathbb H$ onto itself and fixes every
real boundary point. These perturbations span the two real anisotropy
directions modulo similarities.

Fix a bounded hull $K=K_t$ contained in $\{|w|\le R\}$ with $R>0$.
Take a real smooth radial cutoff $\chi_R$ equal to one on $|w|\le2R$
and zero on $|w|\ge4R$, with derivatives of order $j$ bounded by
$C_jR^{-j}$. Set
\[
 V(w)=\chi_R(w)\nu(\bar w-w),\qquad
 \Phi_\epsilon(w)=w+\epsilon V(w),\qquad f=g_t^{-1},\quad W=W_t.
 \tag{AV2}
\]
For sufficiently small $|\epsilon|$ this is a plane diffeomorphism,
fixes $\mathbb R$ pointwise, preserves $\mathbb H$, and equals
$L_\epsilon$ on $K$. Define the compactly supported bounded coefficient
on $\mathbb H$
\[
 \eta(z)=V_{\bar w}(f(z))\,
                \frac{\overline{f'(z)}}{f'(z)}.
 \tag{AV3}
\]
Extend it to the lower half-plane by
$\eta(\bar z)=\overline{\eta(z)}$.

\begin{proposition}[First variation indexed by the original hull prefix]
\label{prop:affine-prefix-variation}
Let
\[
 s_\epsilon=\tfrac12\operatorname{hcap}(L_\epsilon K),\qquad
 U_\epsilon=\widehat g^{\,\epsilon}_{s_\epsilon}
                   (L_\epsilon\gamma(t)),
 \tag{AV4}
\]
where the second expression is interpreted by the tip prime end.
Both quantities are differentiable at $\epsilon=0$, and
\[
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \left.\partial_\epsilon U_\epsilon\right|_0
  &=-\frac2\pi\operatorname{Re}
       \int_{\mathbb H}\frac{\eta(z)}{z-W}\,d^2z,\\
 \left.\partial_\epsilon s_\epsilon\right|_0
  &=\frac1\pi\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb H}\eta(z)\,d^2z.
 \end{aligned}}
 \tag{AV5}
\]
The integrals are absolutely convergent, including at the tip.
Their values are independent of the choice of the stated cutoff.
There are absolute cutoff-dependent constants such that
\[
 |\partial_\epsilon U_\epsilon|_0\le C|\nu|R,
 \qquad |\partial_\epsilon s_\epsilon|_0\le C|\nu|R^2.
 \tag{AV6}
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The derivative of $\Phi_\epsilon$ is uniformly close to the identity,
so this map is a global diffeomorphism for sufficiently small $\epsilon$.
Since $\operatorname{Im}\Phi_\epsilon(x+iy)
=y(1-2\epsilon\operatorname{Re}(\nu)\chi_R(x+iy))$, it preserves
both half-planes. Its image of $K$ agrees with $L_\epsilon K$.

Put
$F_\epsilon=\widehat g^{\,\epsilon}_{s_\epsilon}
\circ\Phi_\epsilon\circ f$ and reflect it across $\mathbb R$.
It is a plane quasiconformal map with principal normalization
\[
 F_\epsilon(z)
  =z+\frac{2(s_\epsilon-t)}z+O(z^{-2})
   \quad (z\to\infty).
 \tag{AV7}
\]
Indeed $\Phi_\epsilon$ is the identity outside a fixed disk, so the
composition there is the composition of the two hydrodynamic conformal
maps. Its Beltrami coefficient is, in the upper half-plane,
\[
 m_\epsilon(z)=
 \frac{\epsilon V_{\bar w}(f(z))}
      {1+\epsilon V_w(f(z))}
       \frac{\overline{f'(z)}}{f'(z)}
   =\epsilon\eta(z)+O(\epsilon^2),
 \tag{AV8}
\]
with the reflected coefficient in the lower half-plane. The remainder
bound is in $L^\infty$, supported on one bounded disk independent of
$\epsilon$. Boundedness of the support follows also directly from
hydrodynamic univalence: the image under $g_t$ of the portion of
$\mathbb H\setminus K$ in a fixed disk is bounded. With the above
cutoff scales, the support is contained in a disk of radius $CR$.

Here is an explicit version of the required differentiability argument.
Let
\[
 \mathcal C h(z)=\frac1\pi\int_{\mathbb C}
                     \frac{h(\zeta)}{z-\zeta}\,d^2\zeta,
 \qquad \mathcal S=\partial_z\mathcal C.
\]
For fixed $p>2$, the Beurling transform is bounded on $L^p$. For
sufficiently small $\epsilon$ the principal solution has
\[
 F_\epsilon=z+\mathcal C h_\epsilon,\qquad
 h_\epsilon=(I-m_\epsilon\mathcal S)^{-1}m_\epsilon.
 \tag{AV9}
\]
The Neumann series and (AV8) imply
$h_\epsilon/\epsilon\to\eta$ in $L^p$, and also in $L^1$ since
all these functions have the same bounded support. On functions
supported in this disk, the Cauchy transform maps $L^p$, $p>2$,
continuously to bounded continuous functions, by H\"older's inequality
at its integrable singularity and the elementary far-field bound.
Consequently
\[
 \frac{F_\epsilon(z)-z}{\epsilon}
       \longrightarrow\mathcal C\eta(z)
 \tag{AV10}
\]
uniformly in $z$, including $z=W\in\mathbb R$.
The boundary identity $U_\epsilon=F_\epsilon(W)$ gives the first
formula (AV5); reflection combines the two half-plane integrals.
The coefficient of $1/z$ in (AV9) is
$\pi^{-1}\int h_\epsilon$, so (AV7) and $L^1$ convergence give
the second formula. Neither step differentiates the physical tip.

All cutoffs produce exactly the same transformed hull $L_\epsilon K$
and distinguished prime end, so their driver and capacity variations
coincide. Finally $\|\eta\|_\infty\le C|\nu|$, its support lies in
$|z|\le CR$, and $|W|\le3R$. Integrating $1/|z-W|$ over this disk
gives the first bound (AV6); its area gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[An explicit constant-anisotropy response]
\label{cor:affine-global-response}
For the boundary-fixed affine deformation (AV1), the driver response
can also be written without a cutoff:
\[
 \boxed{\left.\partial_\epsilon U_\epsilon\right|_0
 =-\frac2\pi\operatorname{Re}\int_{\mathbb H}
 \frac{\nu}{z-W}
 \left(\frac{\overline{f'(z)}}{f'(z)}-1\right)d^2z.}
 \tag{AV11}
\]
The integral is absolutely convergent. This formula remains indexed
by the original hull prefix.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use the actual affine map in place of the cutoff and write
$F_\epsilon=\widehat g^{\,\epsilon}_{s_\epsilon}
\circ L_\epsilon\circ f$ and
$Q_\epsilon=F_\epsilon-L_\epsilon$. Set
$\phi=\overline{f'}/f'$ and
$\mu_\epsilon=\epsilon\nu\phi/(1-\epsilon\nu)$.
The Beltrami equation gives exactly
\[
 (Q_\epsilon)_{\bar z}
       -\mu_\epsilon(Q_\epsilon)_z
 =\epsilon\nu(\phi-1).
 \tag{AV12}
\]
The map $Q_\epsilon$ has real boundary values since $L_\epsilon$
fixes $\mathbb R$, so reflect $Q_\epsilon$ and the two coefficients
across the real line. Its normalization at infinity is
$Q_\epsilon=O(|z|^{-1})$. The right side divided by $\epsilon$,
denoted $m$, is bounded and satisfies $m(z)=O(|z|^{-2})$ at infinity,
because $f'(z)=1+O(z^{-2})$. In particular $m\in L^p\cap L^q$
for fixed $p>2>q>1$.

Writing $\xi_\epsilon=(Q_\epsilon)_{\bar z}$, the normalized
solution of (AV12) is
\[
 \xi_\epsilon=\epsilon(I-\mu_\epsilon\mathcal S)^{-1}m,
 \qquad Q_\epsilon=\mathcal C\xi_\epsilon.
 \tag{AV13}
\]
For small $\epsilon$, the two inverse operators are bounded on both
$L^p$ and $L^q$, so $\xi_\epsilon/\epsilon\to m$ in their
intersection. Near the Cauchy kernel singularity use $p>2$;
at infinity use $q<2$. This proves uniform convergence
$Q_\epsilon/\epsilon\to\mathcal Cm$, including boundary points.
The normalized solution in (AV13) agrees with the geometrically
defined $Q_\epsilon$: their difference solves the homogeneous
Beltrami equation and vanishes at infinity, so factorization through
the normalized quasiconformal homeomorphism reduces it to a bounded
entire function vanishing at infinity. Finally
$L_\epsilon(W)=W$, and reflection yields (AV11).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform Taylor expansion along matched prefixes]
\label{prop:affine-prefix-taylor}
Suppose $K_t\subset\{|z|\le R\}$ for $0\le t\le T$, and use the
same cutoff $V$ from (AV2) for every prefix. Write $D_t$ and $E_t$
for the two first derivatives in (AV5). There are absolute constants
$c,C>0$, depending only on the fixed cutoff profile, such that for
$|\epsilon\nu|\le c$,
\[
 \begin{split}
 \sup_{t\le T}|U_\epsilon(t)-W_t-\epsilon D_t|
       &\le C\epsilon^2|\nu|^2R,\\
 \sup_{t\le T}|s_\epsilon(t)-t-\epsilon E_t|
       &\le C\epsilon^2|\nu|^2R^2,\\
 \sup_{t\le T}|D_t|&\le C|\nu|R,\qquad
 \sup_{t\le T}|E_t|\le C|\nu|R^2.
 \end{split}
 \tag{AV14}
\]
In particular the matched-prefix pair is differentiable in the
uniform path norm at $\epsilon=0$, with continuous first derivative
paths $D,E$. This remains a statement in the original prefix clock.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For every $t\le T$, the coefficients $m_{\epsilon,t}$ in (AV8)
are supported in the same disk of radius $CR$. The bounds
$\|\eta_t\|_\infty\le C|\nu|$ and
$\|m_{\epsilon,t}-\epsilon\eta_t\|_\infty
\le C\epsilon^2|\nu|^2$ hold with constants independent of $t$,
the hulls, and $R$. Fix one $p>2$. Choosing $c$ so that the
Neumann-series inverses on $L^p$ have norm at most two gives
\[
 \|h_{\epsilon,t}-\epsilon\eta_t\|_p
       \le C\epsilon^2|\nu|^2R^{2/p}.
 \tag{AV15}
\]
The difference is supported in the same disk. For a function $k$
supported in that disk, H\"older's inequality gives
$\|\mathcal Ck\|_\infty\le CR^{1-2/p}\|k\|_p$.
Thus (AV15) gives the first remainder in (AV14), even before
evaluating at the moving real point $W_t$. The coefficient at
infinity is estimated in $L^1$:
$\|h_{\epsilon,t}-\epsilon\eta_t\|_1
\le C\epsilon^2|\nu|^2R^2$, proving the second remainder.
The two first-derivative bounds follow by the same estimates.
Continuity of $U_\epsilon(t)$ and $s_\epsilon(t)$, together with
uniform convergence of the difference quotients, gives continuity
of $D_t$ and $E_t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Temporal one-half H\"older regularity of the linear clock response]
\label{cor:affine-clock-holder}
Under the preceding proposition,
\[
 |E_t-E_s|\le C|\nu|R\sqrt{t-s},\qquad 0\le s<t\le T.
 \tag{AV17}
\]
This is a deterministic statement in the original capacity clock.
It does not assert absolute continuity of $E$ or of $s_\epsilon$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Write $\Delta=t-s$. Monotonicity of transformed hull capacity gives
$s_\epsilon(t)-s_\epsilon(s)\ge0$ for both signs of $\epsilon$.
Choose its sign opposite to that of $E_t-E_s$ and apply the two
Taylor remainder bounds. For $0<a\le c/|\nu|$ this gives
\[
 |E_t-E_s|\le\frac{\Delta}{a}
                     +2Ca|\nu|^2R^2.
\]
For $\Delta$ below a sufficiently small constant times $R^2$,
choose $a$ proportional to $\sqrt\Delta/(|\nu|R)$.
For larger $\Delta$, use $|E_t-E_s|\le C|\nu|R^2$ from (AV14).
The case $\nu=0$ is immediate.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Uniform stochastic parameter regularity.}
For a continuous Loewner driver on $[0,T]$, the strip and height
bounds give the deterministic hull-radius estimate
\[
 R_T\le\sup_{t\le T}|W_t|+2\sqrt T.
 \tag{AV16}
\]
Consequently the conditional driving tail (NS4), applied in a
restarted centered half-plane chart, implies conditional exponential
tails for $\sup_{t\le h}|D_t|/(|\nu|\sqrt h)$ and for
$\sqrt{\sup_{t\le h}|E_t|/(|\nu|h)}$, with changed numerical
constants. The two uniform Taylor remainders have respectively
the same Brownian and quadratic size bounds multiplied by
$\epsilon^2|\nu|^2$. These conclusions hold for the unknown
crossing-controlled driver as well as the affine-SLE model. They
give regularity in the \emph{deformation parameter}. Apart from the
separate one-half H\"older bound on $E$, they give no temporal
regularity estimate for $D$ and no finite signed variation of $W$ or $D$.

% Independently checked, 29 October 2026.
\subsection{Temporal regularity of the first affine driver response}
\label{sec:temporal-affine-response}

Use the fixed-cutoff matched-prefix setup above, with $K_t\subset B_R$
for $t\le T$. Let $D_t=\partial_\epsilon U_\epsilon(t)|_0$ in affine
direction $\nu$. For $0\le s<t\le T$ set
\[
 r_{s,t}=\sup_{s\le u\le t}|W_u-W_s|+2\sqrt{t-s}.
\]
\begin{proposition}[The linear affine driver response retains temporal roughness bounds]
\label{prop:temporal-affine-response}
For every integer $N\ge1$, there is $C_N$ depending only on $N$ and the
fixed cutoff profile such that
\[
 |D_t-D_s|\le C_N|\nu|R^{1/(N+1)}r_{s,t}^{N/(N+1)}.
 \tag{TD1}
\]
Consequently if $W$ is H\"older for every exponent below $1/2$, then so is
its matched-prefix first affine response $D$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $p\ge2(N+1)$, taking $p>2$ in the case $N=1$. The principal Beltrami
solution at prefix $s$ is $F_s^\epsilon=\operatorname{id}+\mathcal Ch_{\epsilon,s}$,
with coefficient support in $B_{CR}$ and
\[
 \|h_{\epsilon,s}\|_p\le C_p|\epsilon\nu|R^{2/p},
 \qquad |\epsilon\nu|\le c_p.
\]
The Cauchy transform maps $L^p(\mathbb C)$ to the homogeneous H\"older
class with exponent $1-2/p$, by splitting its difference kernel at the
separation of the two evaluation points. Thus
\[
 |F_s^\epsilon(z)-F_s^\epsilon(w)|
 \le |z-w|+C_p|\epsilon\nu|R^{2/p}|z-w|^{1-2/p}.
 \tag{TD2}
\]
This holds on the closed half-plane, since the Cauchy representation is
continuous there.

The old-chart increment hull between $s$ and $t$ is contained in
$B(W_s,r_{s,t})$: its centered driving oscillation bounds horizontal
extent and its height is at most $2\sqrt{t-s}$. The comparison map
$F_s^\epsilon$ maps it to the increment hull in the deformed chart.
The map agrees on the actual physical hull with the affine deformation,
since the fixed cutoff is one on all prefixes. Equation (TD2) and the
deterministic driver localization estimate applied to this increment give
\[
 |U_\epsilon(t)-U_\epsilon(s)|
 \le3r_{s,t}+C_p|\epsilon\nu|R^{2/p}r_{s,t}^{1-2/p}.
 \tag{TD3}
\]
No stochastic Markov property is used here.

The rational expression for $m_{\epsilon,t}$ and the uniformly convergent
Beurling Neumann series are analytic in the real parameter $\epsilon$
on one interval $|\epsilon\nu|<c_p$, uniformly over prefixes in $B_R$.
More explicitly, their complex analytic extension is obtained by
extending the upper and lower reflected rational coefficient formulas
with the same complex parameter; reflection symmetry is needed only
for real parameters. Their $L^p$ Taylor coefficients are bounded by
$R^{2/p}(C_p|\nu|)^j$. The Cauchy transform estimate gives a uniform
order-$N$ remainder $C_{N,p}|\nu|^{N+1}|\epsilon|^{N+1}R$ for the
matched-prefix driving value.

Choose finite-difference weights $a_1,\dots,a_N$ with
$\sum_{k=1}^N a_kk^j=\mathbf1_{\{j=1\}}$ for $1\le j\le N$.
They give
\[
 D_t=\frac1\epsilon\sum_{k=1}^Na_k
       (U_{k\epsilon}(t)-W_t)
       +O_N(|\epsilon|^N|\nu|^{N+1}R),
 \tag{TD4}
\]
uniformly in $t$. Subtract this formula at $s$ and apply (TD3), obtaining
\[
 |D_t-D_s|\le C_N\left\{
 \frac{r_{s,t}}{|\epsilon|}
 +|\nu|R^{2/p}r_{s,t}^{1-2/p}
 +|\epsilon|^N|\nu|^{N+1}R\right\}.
 \tag{TD5}
\]
For $r_{s,t}/R$ below a sufficiently small constant, choose
$|\epsilon\nu|=c_N(r_{s,t}/R)^{1/(N+1)}$ so all the finite-difference
parameters remain within the analytic interval. The first and third
terms give (TD1); the second is no larger because $2/p\le1/(N+1)$.
For larger $r_{s,t}/R$, (TD1) follows from $\sup|D|\le C|\nu|R$.
The case $\nu=0$ is immediate.

If $W$ is H\"older-$\alpha$ on a fixed horizon with $\alpha<1/2$,
then $r_{s,t}\le C(\omega)(t-s)^\alpha$. Equation (TD1) gives exponent
$\alpha N/(N+1)$ for $D$. Given any $\beta<1/2$, first choose
$\beta<\alpha<1/2$ and then $N$ sufficiently large. Countable choices
of $\alpha,N$ give the simultaneous conclusion.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}[The linear clock response has every temporal exponent below one]
\label{cor:temporal-clock-response-nearly-lipschitz}
For every $0<\theta<2$, the same bounded-hull setup gives
\[
 |E_t-E_s|\le C_\theta|\nu|R^{2-\theta}r_{s,t}^{\theta}.
 \tag{TD6}
\]
If $W$ is H\"older for every exponent below $1/2$, then $E$ is
H\"older for every exponent below $1$. In particular it has finite
$q$-variation for every $q>1$, its sums of squared increments tend
to zero along every sequence of partitions with mesh tending to zero,
and the same is true of mixed sums $\sum\Delta W\Delta E$.
Neither bounded variation nor absolute continuity is asserted.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Choose arbitrarily large $p>2$, and restrict real parameters to a
small interval $|\epsilon\nu|\le\delta_p$ on which the preceding
$L^p$ bounds hold. Put $\alpha=1-2/p$. For $r=r_{s,t}\le R$,
(TD2) bounds the radius of the transformed incremental hull by
$C_p R(r/R)^\alpha$. Its half-plane capacity is at most the square
of this radius. Consequently, uniformly in those real parameters,
\[
 0\le s_\epsilon(t)-s_\epsilon(s)
      \le C_p R^2(r/R)^{2\alpha}.
\]
The same holomorphic parameter argument gives uniform order-$N$
Taylor remainders $C_{N,p}|\epsilon|^{N+1}|\nu|^{N+1}R^2$
for capacity. Apply the finite-difference formula for its first
parameter derivative at both $s$ and $t$. For $a=|\epsilon\nu|$
small enough to accommodate all sample points this yields
\[
 |E_t-E_s|\le C_{N,p}|\nu|R^2
                \{(r/R)^{2\alpha}/a+a^N\}.
\]
Optimize $a$ proportional to $(r/R)^{2\alpha/(N+1)}$, capping
it at the allowed parameter radius. Small $r/R$ gives exponent
$2\alpha N/(N+1)$; larger $r/R$ uses the uniform bound on $E$.
Choose $p,N$ so this exponent exceeds the prescribed $\theta$.
This proves (TD6). The temporal conclusion follows exactly as for
$D$, using $r_{s,t}\le C(\omega)(t-s)^\gamma$ for each
$\gamma<1/2$.

For the final assertions choose a temporal H\"older exponent for
$E$ greater than $1/q$ to obtain finite $q$-variation, or greater
than $1/2$ to obtain vanishing squared-increment sums. For mixed
sums, choose exponents $\gamma<1/2$ for $W$ and $\beta<1$ for
$E$ with $\gamma+\beta>1$; the absolute mixed sum is bounded by
a constant times $T\,\operatorname{mesh}(\pi)^{\gamma+\beta-1}$.
\end{proof}

This is temporal path regularity of the linear affine response under the
original-prefix clock. It proves neither finite signed variation nor
semimartingality, and does not justify differentiating the own-capacity
wrong-coordinate driver with respect to anisotropy.

\paragraph{A useful check.}
For the deterministic vertical slit driven by $W_t=0$, the pure-shear
direction $\nu=i$ gives the response $16\sqrt t/\pi$; this agrees
with the derivative of the standard square-root driver of a slanted
straight ray. Indeed the straight-ray driver $c\sqrt{s}$ has ray angle
$\theta(c)=\tfrac\pi2(1-c/\sqrt{c^2+16})$, so
$\theta'(0)=-\pi/8$, whereas the shear changes the vertical
ray angle by $-2\epsilon+O(\epsilon^2)$. This checks the sign
and constant geometrically. For a pure vertical stretch, symmetry gives zero
driver response and the geometric capacity derivative is $-4t$.

\paragraph{Indexing warning.}
The derivative in (AV5) compares the same original ordered prefix.
It is not the derivative at fixed numerical capacity in the transformed
curve. Inverting $s_\epsilon(t)$ changes the time argument of the
original rough driver. Even a differentiable clock perturbation can
destroy differentiability in $\epsilon$ for a Brownian path.
No fixed-capacity pathwise first variation is claimed.

\paragraph{A model-independent consequence of the crossing radius bound.}
For a continuum family satisfying the conditional crossing assumptions
used earlier, the filled hull during a restarted capacity interval of
length $h$ lies in a disk of radius $R_h$ with
$\mathbb P(R_h>x\sqrt h\mid\mathcal F_\tau)\le C e^{-cx}$,
after changing constants. Apply (AV6) to the restarted half-plane hull.
The affine response of its endpoint driver has conditional moments
$\mathbb E(|\delta U_h|^p\mid\mathcal F_\tau)\le C_p|\nu|^ph^{p/2}$;
the capacity response has moments bounded by $C_p|\nu|^ph^p$.
This concerns affine perturbation of the \emph{restarted chart hull},
not a fixed physical affine perturbation of a rough slit domain.
The latter is encoded by (AB2), rather than a constant chart coefficient.
Neither estimate establishes temporal semimartingality of the driver or
of its first variation.

\paragraph{Literature boundary.}
Sung--Wang~\cite[Theorem 1.1]{sung-wang}, treats a simple chord and
deformation supported away from it. The proof above, using the principal-solution method of
Ahlfors--Bers~\cite{ahlfors-bers}, instead keeps the
bounded filled hull fixed, pulls a compactly supported smooth physical
deformation into its uniformizing half-plane, and uses the principal
Beltrami solution. It does not use a theorem about simple approximants
or omit swallowed positive-area sets from a physical-domain change of
variables.

% Candidate supplement, 29 October 2026. No tracked source modified.
% Independently checked fixed-prefix model calculation, 29 October 2026.
\section{Mean affine response through a reverse Loewner flow}
\label{sec:reverse-affine-response}

Let $W_t=\sqrt\kappa B_t$, $\kappa>0$, be the driver of ordinary
chordal SLE, and let $f_t=g_t^{-1}$. The matched-prefix affine driver
response in direction $\nu$ is
\[
 D_t=-\frac2\pi\operatorname{Re}\nu I_t,\qquad
 I_t=\int_{\mathbb H}\frac{
 \overline{f_t'(W_t+z)}/f_t'(W_t+z)-1}{z}\,dA(z).
\]
The same original hull prefix, rather than the same deformed capacity,
is held fixed.

\paragraph{Absolute integrability and Fubini.}
For $K_t\subset B_R$, the inverse map is hydrodynamically univalent and
$f_t'(z)=1+O(R^2/|z|^2)$ uniformly for $|z|>CR$. Since $|W_t|\le3R$,
\[
 \int_{\mathbb H}\frac{|\overline{f_t'(W_t+z)}/f_t'(W_t+z)-1|}{|z|}
 \,dA(z)\le CR.
\]
Indeed the near part is bounded by $2/|z|$ over a disk of radius $CR$,
and the far part by $CR^2/|z|^3$. For Brownian driving we can take
$R=\sup_{s\le t}|W_s|+2\sqrt t$, so $\mathbb ER\le C_\kappa\sqrt t$.
Thus expectation and the displayed integral may be interchanged. This
uses neither a simple trace nor a zero-area hull.

\paragraph{Reverse-flow representation.}
For fixed $t$ and $0\le r\le t$, put
\[
 H_r(z)=g_{t-r}(f_t(z+W_t))-W_{t-r}.
\]
Then $H_0(z)=z$, $H_t(z)=f_t(z+W_t)$, and pathwise the reverse
Loewner equation is driven by $W_t-W_{t-r}$. This reversed increment
process has Brownian law. Hence the derivative field at time $t$ has
the same law as the spatial derivative of
\[
 dh_r(z)=-\frac2{h_r(z)}dr+\sqrt\kappa\,d\widetilde B_r,
 \qquad h_0(z)=z.
\]
The sign of the Brownian term does not affect its law. Its spatial
derivative satisfies $\partial_rh'_r=2h'_r/h_r^2$. Therefore
\[
 \frac{\overline{h'_t(z)}}{h'_t(z)}
 =\exp\left\{\int_0^t
  2\left(\frac1{\overline{h_r(z)}^2}-\frac1{h_r(z)^2}\right)dr\right\}.
\]
For fixed initial height $y>0$, the reverse image height increases, so
all the coefficients are bounded away from their boundary singularity
on finite time intervals.

Define
\[
 u(t,z)=\mathbb E\frac{\overline{h'_t(z)}}{h'_t(z)}.
\]
It is the bounded Feynman--Kac solution, in the stochastic semigroup
sense, of
\[
 \partial_tu=\mathcal Lu+qu,\quad u(0,z)=1,
\]
where
\[
 \mathcal L=\frac\kappa2\partial_x^2-
       \frac{2x}{x^2+y^2}\partial_x+
       \frac{2y}{x^2+y^2}\partial_y,
 \qquad q(z)=\frac{8ixy}{(x^2+y^2)^2}.
\]
No boundary condition at $y=0$ is inserted: the stochastic representation
from each interior starting point specifies the solution. Uniqueness among bounded classical solutions, or within the stated
stochastic semigroup class, follows by stopped It\^o/Feynman--Kac on $y\ge y_0>0$ and
localization in $x$, since the phase potential is purely imaginary.
Consequently
\[
 \mathbb ED_t=-\frac2\pi\operatorname{Re}
 \left\{\nu\int_{\mathbb H}\frac{u(t,z)-1}{z}\,dA(z)\right\}.
\]
The integral is absolutely convergent by the preceding Fubini bound.

\paragraph{Symmetry and scaling.}
Reflection of Brownian driving gives
$u(t,-\bar z)=\overline{u(t,z)}$, so the last integral is purely
imaginary. Brownian scaling makes it $iJ_\kappa\sqrt t$ for a real
constant $J_\kappa$. Thus
\[
 \mathbb ED_t=c_\kappa\operatorname{Im}(\nu)\sqrt t,
 \qquad c_\kappa=2J_\kappa/\pi.
\]
The first-order mean vertical-stretch response vanishes, whereas a shear
response is allowed by symmetry. Determining whether $c_6$ is zero,
and more generally computing $c_\kappa$, is a concrete next model task.
This calculation does not determine temporal semimartingality of $D$
or of the finite-affine wrong-coordinate driver.

\paragraph{An interior cancellation, not a boundary conclusion.}
Let $\mathcal L^*$ denote the formal transpose under the bilinear
integral pairing, so the multiplication potential remains $q$, not
its complex conjugate. Direct differentiation gives the pointwise identity
\[
 (\mathcal L^*+q)\frac1z=\frac{\kappa-6}{z^3},\qquad z\in\mathbb H.
\]
At $\kappa=6$ the interior term vanishes. This does not justify
integrating by parts over the full half-plane: the test $1/z$ is
singular at the boundary and has a noncompact tail. Boundary and
infinity fluxes have not been controlled. In particular no vanishing
or nonvanishing of $c_6$ is deduced. Studying those fluxes is a precise
next question for the affine-SLE$_6$ model.

\section{Coordinate-compatible exit clocks before semimartingality}
\label{sec:adaptive-exit-clocks}

The upper and lower conditional oscillation bounds already proved for the
continuum family have a stronger clock consequence. It is compatible with
working in a wrong affine structure: it assumes neither a signed budget
nor a canonical semimartingale decomposition. All constants may depend
on the fixed affine distortion through the crossing constants.

Let $X$ be a continuous adapted process on $[0,\infty)$ in a usual
filtration. Suppose constants $C,c,\delta,p>0$, with $0<p<1$, satisfy,
for every finite stopping time $\tau$, every $h>0$, and every $x\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \mathbb P\{\sup_{u\le h}|X_{\tau+u}-X_\tau|>x\sqrt h
       \mid\mathcal F_\tau\}&\le Ce^{-cx},\label{eq:exit-upper-input}\\
 \mathbb P\{\sup_{u\le h}|X_{\tau+u}-X_\tau|>\delta\sqrt h
       \mid\mathcal F_\tau\}&\ge p.\label{eq:exit-lower-input}
\end{align}
For the current exploration assumptions, these follow from the upper
crossing estimate, corrected ray-deletion locality, and strong DMP.
The rough-state admissibility qualifications of those results remain
in force. Physical translation covariance is not needed for this deduction.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform Brownian-order exit-time control]
\label{prop:diffusive-exit-time}
For $r>0$, put
\[
 \xi_r(\tau)=\inf\{u\ge0:|X_{\tau+u}-X_\tau|\ge r\}.
\]
There are constants $a_i>0$, depending only on the input constants,
such that, conditionally on $\mathcal F_\tau$,
\begin{align}
 \mathbb P\{\xi_r(\tau)\le h\mid\mathcal F_\tau\}
   &\le C\exp(-cr/\sqrt h),\label{eq:exit-fast-tail}\\
 \mathbb P\{\xi_r(\tau)>h\mid\mathcal F_\tau\}
   &\le (1-p)^{\lfloor\delta^2h/(4r^2)\rfloor},
       \label{eq:exit-slow-tail}\\
 a_1r^2\le\mathbb E[\xi_r(\tau)\mid\mathcal F_\tau]
   &\le a_2r^2,\qquad
 \mathbb E[\exp(a_3\xi_r(\tau)/r^2)\mid\mathcal F_\tau]
   \le a_4.\label{eq:exit-moments}
\end{align}
In particular, exits occur in finite time almost surely.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For the fast bound, the exit event implies that the supremum on $[0,h]$
is at least $r$. Apply \eqref{eq:exit-upper-input} first at thresholds
$r-\varepsilon$ and then let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
For the slow bound, split time into blocks of length
$d r^2$, where $d=4/\delta^2$. If the exit has not occurred by the end
of a block, both the path and its value at the start of that block lie
in the original interval $[X_\tau-r,X_\tau+r]$. Their displacement
throughout the block is at most $2r=\delta\sqrt{d r^2}$.
The conditional lower bound at the block's starting stopping time
therefore bounds the conditional survival probability by $1-p$.
Iteration proves \eqref{eq:exit-slow-tail}. Integrating that tail gives
the upper mean and an exponential moment. Choose $a>0$ small enough
that $Ce^{-c/\sqrt a}\le1/2$. The fast bound gives
$\mathbb P\{\xi_r(\tau)>ar^2\mid\mathcal F_\tau\}\ge1/2$,
hence the lower mean bound $ar^2/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Adaptive quadratic activity is nondegenerate]
\label{prop:adaptive-quadratic-activity}
Define $\sigma^r_0=0$ and successive exits by
\[
 \sigma^r_{k+1}=\inf\{t\ge\sigma^r_k:
                   |X_t-X_{\sigma^r_k}|\ge r\},\qquad
 \nu_r=r^2\sum_{k\ge1}\delta_{\sigma^r_k}.
\]
The exit times do not accumulate on a finite interval. There are fixed
$0<a<b<\infty$ and $A,\alpha>0$ such that for every deterministic
interval $I=[s,t]$, $h=t-s>0$,
\[
 \mathbb P\{\nu_r(I)<ah\ \hbox{or}\ \nu_r(I)>bh\}
       \le A\exp(-\alpha h/r^2).
 \label{eq:exit-count-concentration}
\]
Consequently, on one probability-one event, the measures
$\nu_{2^{-m}}$ are relatively compact on every finite interval, and
every subsequential weak limit $\nu$ satisfies
\[
 a\,dt\le d\nu\le b\,dt
 \label{eq:adaptive-clock-density}
\]
(after decreasing $a$ and increasing $b$ if necessary).
These measures are realized squared increments on the corresponding
exit partitions, not a claimed canonical quadratic variation.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Finite-time nonaccumulation follows from continuity: infinitely many
oscillations of size $r$ on a compact interval contradict uniform
continuity. Every exit time is finite by the preceding proposition,
and every completed exit increment has square exactly $r^2$.

Write $D_k=(\sigma^r_{k+1}-\sigma^r_k)/r^2$.
For some $\lambda>0$ and $K<\infty$, the preceding proposition gives
\[
 \mathbb E[e^{\lambda D_k}\mid\mathcal F_{\sigma^r_k}]\le K.
 \label{eq:exit-positive-laplace}
\]
Also its fast-tail proof supplies $a_0>0$ with
$\mathbb P\{D_k>a_0\mid\mathcal F_{\sigma^r_k}\}\ge1/2$.
Thus, for every $\eta>0$,
\[
 \mathbb E[e^{-\eta D_k}\mid\mathcal F_{\sigma^r_k}]
 \le\rho:=\tfrac12(1+e^{-\eta a_0})<1.
 \label{eq:exit-negative-laplace}
\]
Iteration and exponential Markov inequalities give, for sums of $n$
successive full exit durations, the bounds
\[
 \mathbb P\{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^nD_j>H\mid\hbox{initial past}\}
       \le K^ne^{-\lambda H},\qquad
 \mathbb P\{\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^nD_j\le H\mid\hbox{initial past}\}
       \le\rho^ne^{\eta H}.
 \label{eq:exit-renewal-bounds}
\]
No independence of the durations is used.

To count exits in a fixed interval starting at $s$, first wait for the
first global exit $\theta\ge s$. The residual time $R=\theta-s$
also has a uniform conditional exponential moment at scale $r^2$.
Indeed, before $\theta$, the process remains within $r$ of the previous
exit value, hence within $2r$ of $X_s$. Repeating the slow-tail proof
with that larger confinement band gives
$\mathbb P\{R>u\mid\mathcal F_s\}\le
 (1-p)^{\lfloor\delta^2u/(16r^2)\rfloor}$.
Consequently for a sufficiently small common $\lambda$,
$\mathbb E[e^{\lambda R/r^2}\mid\mathcal F_s]\le K_0$.
If an interval has at most $n$ exits, its residual duration plus $n$
full durations is at least $h$. If it has at least $n+1$ exits, $n$ full
durations after its first exit fit inside $h$. Thus
\[
 \mathbb P\{\nu_r(I)/r^2\le n\}\le K_0K^ne^{-\lambda h/r^2},
 \qquad
 \mathbb P\{\nu_r(I)/r^2\ge n+1\}\le\rho^ne^{\eta h/r^2}.
\]
Choose $n$ respectively as a sufficiently small and sufficiently large
multiple of $h/r^2$. Rounding and bounded values of $h/r^2$ are absorbed
in $A$. This proves \eqref{eq:exit-count-concentration}.

For dyadic $r$, the right side of that estimate is summable for every
fixed rational interval. Borel--Cantelli and a countable intersection
give eventual upper and lower mass bounds on every rational interval.
The upper bounds give relative compactness of finite measures on compact
intervals. For any weak subsequential limit, use rational open intervals
for the upper mass bound and rational closed intervals for the lower
bound, applying the Portmanteau inequalities. Approximation by rational
endpoints gives \eqref{eq:adaptive-clock-density}; in particular the
limits have no atoms. Finite endpoint conventions in the counts do not
affect the conclusion, since each atom of $\nu_r$ has mass $r^2$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{What is gained and what is still missing.}
The continuum axioms already supply nonzero finite quadratic activity
along observable stopping-time partitions, with subsequential activity
clocks comparable to capacity. This is stronger information than merely
stating a variation index. It does not establish uniqueness of the clock,
convergence along deterministic partitions, semimartingality, or variance
six. Even uniqueness of a quadratic-activity clock could not by itself
establish semimartingality: the earlier Brownian-plus-infinite-variation
H\"older example remains a warning about missing signed cancellation.

At exit times the displacement is $\varepsilon_kr$, with
$\varepsilon_k\in\{-1,1\}$. The remaining observable signed quantity is
\[
 r\sum_{k<N}\left|\mathbb E[\varepsilon_k
                         \mid\mathcal F_{\sigma^r_k}]\right|.
\]
The new clock estimate controls the number of summands by order $T/r^2$;
it does not improve the resulting crude bound of order $T/r$ on this
signed budget. A useful next question is how the predictable exit-sign
bias changes under a candidate constant affine normalization, or whether
locality yields cancellations for a corrected observable depending on
the transported Beltrami field. Finite signed variation of the raw driver
in every affine coordinate system is not being assumed or recommended.

\section{Affine capacity clocks and the limits of scaling arguments}
\label{sec:affine-clock-obstructions}

\subsection{Global affine capacity comparison does not control clock increments}
\label{sec:global-affine-capacity}

The affine clock at least has a deterministic global comparison. This
uses only the geometric capacity theorem of Lalley--Lawler--Narayanan
\cite[Theorem 1]{lln-capacity}, not any regularity of the trace or driver.

\begin{proposition}[Capacity distortion by a fixed affine map]
\label{prop:global-affine-capacity}
Let $L(x+iy)=\alpha x+\beta y+i\delta y$, where
$\alpha,\delta>0$ and $\beta\in\mathbb R$. There are constants
$0<c_L\le C_L<\infty$ such that every bounded half-plane hull $K$
satisfies
\[
 c_L\operatorname{hcap}(K)\le\operatorname{hcap}(LK)
          \le C_L\operatorname{hcap}(K).
 \tag{GC1}
\]
Consequently, if $\operatorname{hcap}(K_t)=2t$, its affine clock
$s(t)=\operatorname{hcap}(LK_t)/2$ satisfies $c_Lt\le s(t)\le C_Lt$.
The constants can be chosen uniformly when $L$ ranges over a compact
set of invertible half-plane-preserving linear maps.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a family of disks $\{B_j\}$ with bounded radii, the elementary
Vitali selection argument gives, for every $\lambda\ge1$,
\[
 \left|\bigcup_j\lambda B_j\right|
       \le(2\lambda+3)^2\left|\bigcup_jB_j\right|.
 \tag{GC2}
\]
Indeed one can select disjoint disks such that each original disk
meets a selected disk of at least half its radius. Its $\lambda$-dilate
then lies in the $(2\lambda+3)$-dilate of that selected disk. Sum their
areas; arbitrary families reduce to countable subfamilies of the open
union.

Let $m,M$ be the smallest and largest singular values of $L$, and
$d=\det L$. Put
\[
 H(K)=\bigcup_{z\in K}B(z,\operatorname{Im}z),
 \qquad \operatorname{hsiz}(K)=|H(K)|.
\]
For $z=x+iy$, the ellipse $LB(z,y)$ contains $B(Lz,my)$ and is
contained in $B(Lz,My)$, whereas the corresponding disk in $H(LK)$
is $B(Lz,\delta y)$. Since $m\le\delta\le M$, (GC2) yields
\[
 \frac{d}{(2M/\delta+3)^2}\operatorname{hsiz}(K)
 \le\operatorname{hsiz}(LK)
 \le d(2\delta/m+3)^2\operatorname{hsiz}(K).
 \tag{GC3}
\]
The cited theorem gives universal positive $a,b$ with
$a\operatorname{hsiz}(K)\le\operatorname{hcap}(K)
\le b\operatorname{hsiz}(K)$. Apply it to both $K$ and $LK$.
This proves (GC1), the clock assertion, and local uniformity in $L$.
\end{proof}

This is a comparison from the original source time zero, not an
increment estimate. After mapping out $K_t$, the transported affine
map is quasiconformal with a history-dependent field; it is no longer
the original linear map. Applying (GC1) to that increment would be
invalid. Even for increasing continuous functions, $s(t)\asymp t$
does not imply absolute continuity: $s(t)=t+\epsilon C(t)$, with
$C$ a Cantor staircase supported on $[1,2]$, has a singular part and
still satisfies such a comparison. Determining whether the actual
affine-SLE clock has a singular component remains open here.

% Research fragment, 29 October 2026. No claim of actual clock singularity.
\subsection{A necessary clock regularity condition for wrong-coordinate semimartingality}
\label{sec:affine-clock-semimartingale-test}

Let $\eta_s$ be chordal SLE$_6$ in $\mathbb H$, parametrized by
$\operatorname{hcap}(K_s)=2s$, and let $B_s$ be its ordinary Brownian driver
divided by $\sqrt6$. Let $L$ be an orientation-preserving real-linear
self-map of $\mathbb H$. Put
\[
 \tau(s)=\tfrac12\operatorname{hcap}(LK_s),\qquad
 \gamma_{\tau(s)}=L\eta_s,
\]
and denote the ordinary Loewner driver of $\gamma$ by $W_t$.
We assume continuous Loewner generation, strictly growing hulls, and
infinite lifetimes for both parametrizations. Thus $\tau$ and its inverse
are continuous increasing clocks. Completed natural filtrations are used.

\begin{proposition}[Semimartingality forces absolute continuity of the affine clock]
\label{prop:affine-clock-ac-necessary}
Suppose $W=M+A$ is a continuous semimartingale in its natural
filtration and its martingale bracket $C=\langle M\rangle$ satisfies
\[
 c\,dt\le dC_t\le C_0\,dt
 \tag{AC1}
\]
on the relevant localized intervals, with deterministic constants
$0<c\le C_0<\infty$. Then $\tau$ is absolutely continuous in $s$.
There exists a Brownian-predictable $H$ such that, locally,
\[
 M_{\tau(s)}=\int_0^s H_u\,dB_u,
 \qquad
 c\,d\tau(s)\le H_s^2ds\le C_0\,d\tau(s).
 \tag{AC2}
\]
In particular, a nonzero singular component of $d\tau$ would exclude
semimartingality of $W$, whenever the crossing/locality hypotheses
needed for (AC1) hold.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An initial segment of $W$ reconstructs the corresponding ordered
Loewner trace and hull. Applying $L^{-1}$ reconstructs the original
SLE segment, its original capacity, and its driver. Conversely the
Brownian driver up to $s$ reconstructs the transformed segment and
its driver up to $\tau(s)$. Thus, with the usual completions,
\[
 \mathcal F^W_{\tau(s)}=\mathcal F^B_s.
 \tag{AC3}
\]
This assertion retains the ordered trace and distinguished tip; an
unlabelled hull or frontier alone is not substituted for that state.

For every fixed $s$, $\tau(s)$ is a stopping time in the $W$
filtration: at observed time $t$ one can reconstruct the original
capacity $\tau^{-1}(t)$, and
$\{\tau(s)\le t\}=\{\tau^{-1}(t)\ge s\}$.
Consequently $N_s=M_{\tau(s)}$ is a continuous local martingale
in the time-changed filtration, which is the Brownian filtration
by (AC3). Localize both clocks and the martingale in the usual way.
The Brownian martingale representation theorem gives
$N_s=\int_0^sH_u\,dB_u$, and therefore
$C_{\tau(s)}=\int_0^sH_u^2du$.

The measure inequality (AC1), applied pathwise to each clock interval,
gives
\[
 c\,d\tau\le d(C\circ\tau)\le C_0\,d\tau.
\]
Its middle measure equals $H_s^2ds$. The lower inequality forces
$d\tau\ll ds$ and proves all assertions.
\end{proof}

The already established upper conditional driving moment bound yields
the upper inequality in (AC1) once semimartingality is assumed.
The ray-deletion locality lower tail yields the lower inequality.
Thus (AC1) uses precisely those explicit continuum crossing and
prime-end admissibility hypotheses; it is not a consequence of
semimartingality alone. No singularity of the actual affine clock has
been proved here. Absolute continuity of $\tau$ is only necessary,
not sufficient for semimartingality. It also does not imply absolute
continuity of its inverse: $\tau'$ might vanish on a positive-measure
set. Global comparisons $c_Ls\le\tau(s)\le C_Ls$ do not remove this
possibility or give an increment comparison.

\subsection{Why scaling and a trivial germ do not regularize the bracket}
\label{sec:elliptic-selfsimilar-counterexample}

Here is a process-level obstruction to strengthening the previous
scaling lemma without its initial-bracket premise. It is not a
proposed exploration family satisfying locality and DMP.

\begin{proposition}[A self-similar elliptic martingale without an initial bracket tangent]
\label{prop:selfsimilar-elliptic-no-tangent}
Let $B$ be standard Brownian motion and define
\[
 X_t=\int_0^t\sqrt{2+\sin(B_s/\sqrt s)}\,dB_s,
 \qquad t\ge0.
 \tag{AC4}
\]
The integrand at zero may be chosen arbitrarily. Then $X$ is a
continuous martingale in its own natural filtration, its initial germ
is trivial, and it has exact Brownian scaling. Its bracket satisfies
\[
 dt\le d\langle X\rangle_t\le3dt.
 \tag{AC5}
\]
Nevertheless $\langle X\rangle_t/t$ has no limit in probability as
$t\downarrow0$. It satisfies conditional Brownian-scale upper
oscillation tails and a positive conditional lower oscillation tail
uniformly over stopping times.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The integrand is bounded and progressive, so the integral is a
square-integrable martingale on each finite horizon. A martingale
remains a martingale in its own smaller natural filtration; its
bracket is the pathwise quadratic variation and hence remains
\[
 Q_t=\int_0^t[2+\sin(B_s/\sqrt s)]ds.
\]
This proves (AC5). The natural $X$ filtration is contained in the
Brownian filtration, so its completed initial germ is trivial.
For every $a>0$, substitution $\widetilde B_u=B_{au}/\sqrt a$
in the stochastic integral proves
$(a^{-1/2}X_{at})_{t\ge0}\stackrel{\rm law}=(X_t)_{t\ge0}$.
The same substitution shows $Q_t/t$ has the fixed distribution of
$Q_1$.

This distribution is nondegenerate. Fix small $\epsilon>0$.
On $[\epsilon,1]$, Brownian paths have positive probability of lying
in a sufficiently small uniform neighborhood of either continuous
function $s\mapsto\pi\sqrt s/2$ or its negative. This follows from
Brownian full support after extending each function continuously to
zero. On these two events the integral of the sine over
$[\epsilon,1]$ is close respectively to $1-\epsilon$ and
$-(1-\epsilon)$, whereas its contribution on $[0,\epsilon]$ has
absolute value at most $\epsilon$. Thus $Q_1$ is nonconstant.

If $Q_t/t$ converged in probability at zero, an almost surely
converging subsequence would make its limit measurable in the
Brownian initial germ. The limit would be deterministic, contradicting
the fixed nondegenerate distribution. Hence there is no such limit.

For completeness, the upper bracket bound and the exponential
martingale inequality give conditional Gaussian upper tails for
$\sup_{u\le h}|X_{\sigma+u}-X_\sigma|/\sqrt h$ at every
stopping time $\sigma$. For the lower tail, restart the martingale
at $\sigma$ and apply Dambis--Dubins--Schwarz. Its bracket over
$[\sigma,\sigma+h]$ is at least $h$, so its maximum dominates
the maximum of a Brownian motion over $[0,h]$. This gives any fixed
positive Brownian lower-tail probability, conditionally on the
past. Passing to the natural $X$ filtration preserves both bounds
by conditional expectation.
\end{proof}

This example rules out the shortcut ``exact scaling + Brownian
germ triviality + bounded elliptic bracket implies Brownian
driving.'' It does not exclude the possibility that the actual
wrong-coordinate affine-SLE driver is a semimartingale whose bracket
density oscillates on every logarithmic scale near zero.
